## Turn 4 — User  `2026-09-01 13:04:54 PDT`

Base directory for this skill: /home/dwaddington/ai-native-storage-certus/.claude/skills/profile-compare-with

Compare the performance of the current branch against a reference branch using `certus-api-bench_v2.py`. The benchmark runs on the local node with identical parameters for both branches, producing a side-by-side comparison.

## Arguments

- `branch-name` (optional): The branch to compare against. Defaults to `unstable`.

## Interactive Configuration

Before starting, ask the user for the **server profile** to use:

- **Profile name**: Which `CERTUS_PROFILE` to build with (e.g., `full`, `full-remote`, `full-fs-block`, `full-kernel-block`).
  Available profiles can be listed from `apps/certus-server-yaml/profiles/`.
- **Cargo features** (optional): Extra features to pass (e.g., `--features rdma`). Default: none beyond what the profile implies.

If the user doesn't specify, default to `CERTUS_PROFILE=full`.

## Workflow

### 1. Pre-flight

- Record the current branch name: `CURRENT_BRANCH=$(git branch --show-current)`
- Record the comparison branch: `COMPARE_BRANCH` (from argument, default `unstable`)
- Check for uncommitted changes. If any exist, auto-commit them:
  ```bash
  git add -A && git commit -m "wip: auto-save before performance comparison"
  ```
- Verify both branches exist locally (fetch if needed):
  ```bash
  git fetch origin $COMPARE_BRANCH 2>/dev/null || true
  ```

### 2. Hardware Detection (if profile requires real SSDs)

If the selected profile uses a real block device backend (any profile other than those using `--device-path`), detect available NVMe SSDs:

```bash
# Detect NVMe SSDs via lspci (Samsung, Intel, Kioxia common vendor IDs)
# or use the SPDK-detected devices from a quick probe
lspci -Dnd ::0108 | sort
```

**Select up to 4 SSDs** from those available. For each device, determine its NUMA node:

```bash
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/<PCI_ADDR>/numa_node
```

**NUMA warning**: If any selected SSD is outside NUMA node 0, print:
```
⚠️  WARNING: SSD <PCI_ADDR> is on NUMA node <N> (not node 0).
   Performance may be reduced due to cross-NUMA memory access.
   Consider using only NUMA-0 devices for accurate benchmarking.
```

Pass the selected devices as:
```bash
--device-pci <PCI1> --device-pci <PCI2> --device-pci <PCI3> --device-pci <PCI4>
```

If the profile can use `--device-path /dev/null` (no real SSD needed), use that instead and skip hardware detection.

### 3. Build and Benchmark Current Branch

- Build release:
  ```bash
  CERTUS_PROFILE=$PROFILE cargo build -p certus-server-yaml --release $FEATURES
  ```
- Start the server:
  ```bash
  target/release/certus-server-yaml $DEVICE_FLAGS --format --memory-tier-size 4G &
  SERVER_PID=$!
  sleep 5
  ```
- Run the benchmark:
  ```bash
  cd apps/python
  python3 certus-api-bench_v2.py \
      --clients 1 \
      --num-objects 32 \
      --iterations 20 \
      --block-size 4M \
      --batch-size 16 \
      --pipeline-depth 4 \
      --memory-tier-size 2G
  ```
- Capture the full output (including throughput numbers) to `CURRENT_RESULTS`
- Stop the server:
  ```bash
  kill $SERVER_PID && wait $SERVER_PID 2>/dev/null
  ```

### 4. Switch to Comparison Branch, Build and Benchmark

- Checkout the comparison branch:
  ```bash
  git checkout $COMPARE_BRANCH
  ```
- Build release (same profile and features):
  ```bash
  CERTUS_PROFILE=$PROFILE cargo build -p certus-server-yaml --release $FEATURES
  ```
- Start the server (same device flags and memory-tier size):
  ```bash
  target/release/certus-server-yaml $DEVICE_FLAGS --format --memory-tier-size 4G &
  SERVER_PID=$!
  sleep 5
  ```
- Run the same benchmark with identical parameters (including `--memory-tier-size 2G`)
- Capture output to `COMPARE_RESULTS`
- Stop the server

### 5. Return to Original Branch

```bash
git checkout $CURRENT_BRANCH
```

### 6. Report

Print a comparison table with the key metrics extracted from both runs:

```
================================================================
Performance Comparison: $CURRENT_BRANCH vs $COMPARE_BRANCH
================================================================

Profile: $PROFILE  Features: $FEATURES
Devices: $DEVICE_FLAGS
Benchmark: certus-api-bench_v2.py
Parameters: --clients 1 --num-objects 32 --iterations 20 --block-size 4M --batch-size 16 --pipeline-depth 4

                          Current ($CURRENT_BRANCH)    Reference ($COMPARE_BRANCH)    Delta
Populate throughput:      X.XX GB/s                    Y.YY GB/s                      +/-Z.Z%
Hot lookup throughput:    X.XX GB/s                    Y.YY GB/s                      +/-Z.Z%
Cold lookup throughput:   X.XX GB/s                    Y.YY GB/s                      +/-Z.Z%
Hot lookup latency (p50): X.XX ms                     Y.YY ms                        +/-Z.Z%
Hot lookup latency (p99): X.XX ms                     Y.YY ms                        +/-Z.Z%
```

Mark regressions (>5% slower) with ⚠️ and improvements (>5% faster) with ✓.

## Important Notes

- If the comparison branch does not compile with the selected profile, report the build error and skip its benchmark.
- Always return to the original branch at the end, even if something fails.
- The benchmark parameters above are defaults — the user may override them in their invocation message.
- Both builds use the SAME profile and features to ensure apples-to-apples comparison.
- When using real SSDs, always pass `--format` to start fresh (avoids stale data affecting results).
- The `--device-path /dev/null` mode only exercises the hot path (memory-tier). Use real SSDs to benchmark the cold path (SSD read + promote).


ARGUMENTS: unstable

Both runs complete, server stopped, back on `evolve-dispatcher`. Here's the comparison.

```
================================================================
Performance Comparison: evolve-dispatcher vs unstable
================================================================

Profile:  full          Features: (none)
Devices:  4× NUMA-0 Intel NVMe (0000:61/62/63/64:00.0)
Benchmark: certus-api-bench_v2.py
Params:   --clients 1 --num-objects 32 --iterations 20 --block-size 4M
          --batch-size 16 --pipeline-depth 4 --memory-tier-size 2G
Server:   --format --memory-tier-size 4G

                          evolve-dispatcher   unstable        Delta
  Populate throughput      5.31 GB/s          5.33 GB/s       -0.4%   (parity)
  Hot lookup throughput   15.06 GB/s         14.63 GB/s       +2.9%
  Cold lookup throughput  11.56 GB/s         10.65 GB/s       +8.5%  ✓
  Hot lookup  p50          1075.2 us           994.3 us       +8.1%  ⚠️ (slower)
  Hot lookup  p99          2405.8 us          3266.6 us      -26.3%  ✓ (faster)
  Cold lookup p50          1448.5 us          1466.4 us       -1.2%   (parity)
  Cold lookup p99          1862.9 us          2263.5 us      -17.7%  ✓ (faster)
  Cold lookup avg          1348.9 us          1502.5 us      -10.2%  ✓ (faster)
  Cold/Hot ratio               1.2x              1.4x
================================================================
```

**Read of the results:**

- **Cold path is the clear win.** `evolve-dispatcher` delivers +8.5% cold-lookup throughput, -10% average cold latency, and a -18% cold p99 tail. The cold path (SSD read + promote) is what dispatcher changes touch, and it moved consistently in the right direction — the Cold/Hot ratio tightened from 1.4x to 1.2x.
- **Tail latency improved on both lookup paths** (hot p99 -26%, cold p99 -18%), which lines up with the recent `perf(dispatcher): cut single-key cold load p99 latency by 57%` work on this branch.
- **Populate is at parity** (-0.4%, noise) — unchanged, as expected since it's the write path.
- **Hot p50 is 8% higher on the branch** — flagged with ⚠️, but this is the one metric moving against the branch while its own p99 improved 26%. That pattern (worse median, better tail) in a single 20-iteration round is most likely run-to-run variance rather than a real regression.

**Caveat:** each side is a single benchmark round (N=1), so deltas under ~10% sit inside run-to-run noise — treat the hot-p50 and populate numbers as parity. The cold-throughput and tail-latency gains are large enough and directionally consistent with the branch's stated intent to be credible signal. If you want to firm up the hot-p50 question, I can run 3–5 repeats per branch and report medians.

I left you back on `evolve-dispatcher`; raw output is in `/tmp/profcmp/` if you want the full dumps.

## Turn 60 — Assistant  `2026-09-01 13:46:45 PDT`  _(in:2 out:2,607 cw:3,089 cr:142,879 cost:$0.0936)_

The run **crashed at conversation 121/400 — it did not complete, so there is no valid throughput number to compare against the prior head-to-head.** Here's the full picture.

## What happened

vLLM's `EngineCore` died with an `AssertionError` (`assert transfer_result.success` in `offloading/worker.py:343`), triggered by a **Check→Pin eviction race** in the certus-spdk load path:

```
[certus-shmq] PIN FAILURE  key=… (Check said present, Pin says gone — eviction race)   ×12
[certus-shmq] LOAD FAILURE key=… (this key was Check-hit and Pinned in prepare_load)    ×12
→ transfer_result.success = False  →  vLLM asserts  →  EngineDeadError
```

The sequence: the connector **Checks** a key is resident → decides to load it → **Pins** it — but the server's background evictor **demoted/evicted the entry in between**, so the Pin fails ("gone"), the load fails, and the connector returns a failed transfer. vLLM treats any failed transfer as fatal and kills the engine.

**Why it fired here:** GPU KV cache was at 98.9% and the memory-tier spiked to **99.7%** (evict threshold 0.6, 5,250 demotion events on a ~200ms loop). Under that demotion pressure the Check→Pin window got hit. It ran clean for 121 convs (~5.8 gen/s), then a single 12-key burst killed it. The **server logged no error** — it kept demoting healthily; the race is in the check-vs-evict ordering, not a server fault.

## This looks like a regression, not just bad luck

- The **same config completed** on the prior head-to-head (`cl60-shmq-20260831`: 400 convs / 60 sessions / 13G / evict 0.6 → **5.4 gen/s, rc=0, 17,429 gens**).
- `evolve-dispatcher` heavily reworked exactly the implicated paths — most tellingly the commit **`perf(dispatcher): batch populate, shard dispatch-map/memory-tier, tune cold pipeline`**, plus `memory-tier/src/lib.rs` (+228), `dispatch-map` state, and the cold-load pipeline. Sharding the dispatch-map and memory-tier is a very plausible way to open a Check→Pin atomicity window that didn't exist before.

## Comparison status

| | gen/s | result |
|---|---|---|
| Prior head-to-head (certus-shmq) | 5.4 | completed (17,429 gens) |
| Prior head-to-head (cputier fix#2) | 3.18 | completed |
| **evolve-dispatcher (this run)** | ~5.8 partial | **CRASHED @ conv 121/400** — void |

The ~5.8 gen/s was tracking slightly *above* the prior 5.4 up to the crash, but it's a partial rate under a still-filling cache, **not comparable** to a completed run.

## Suggested next steps

1. **Fix the real bug** (recommended): make the load path tolerate a failed pin as a cache-miss (recompute) instead of returning a fatal failure, and/or restore check+pin atomicity across the new dispatch-map/memory-tier shards. The connector already *detects* the race — it just handles it fatally.
2. **Confirm it's a regression**: re-run the identical config on `unstable` (~20 min to crash point / ~55 min full). If unstable completes, that pins the regression to this branch.
3. **Quick unblock to get a number** (doesn't fix the bug): bump `--memory-tier-size` or set evict threshold to 0 so demotion pressure never triggers the race — but that diverges from the baseline config.

How do you want to proceed — investigate/fix the race, or first re-run `unstable` to confirm the regression? I'll also save this crash + root cause to memory so it's on record.

Want me to save the finding and/or kick off the `unstable` confirmation run?

Decisive result — and it flips the conclusion in an important way.

**`unstable` hit the *identical* Check→Pin race (same 12-key PIN-failure burst) at conv 241 — but the engine SURVIVED it.** No LOAD failures, no `EngineDeadError`; progress continued right through the burst (conv 241 → 11,438 → 11,746 generations, still 60 in flight, container up 36 min, server running). It's still going toward completion.

Contrast with `evolve-dispatcher`, where the same 12 PIN failures became **12 LOAD failures → `transfer_result.success=False` → `EngineDeadError`** at conv 121.

## What this means

| | Check→Pin race occurs? | PIN→LOAD cascade? | Engine | Crash conv |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **unstable** | Yes (12 at conv 241) | **No** (LOAD=0) | **survived** | — |
| **evolve-dispatcher** | Yes (12 at conv 121) | **Yes** (LOAD=12) | **died** | 121 |

The underlying race (evictor demotes a key in the Check→Pin window) is **pre-existing in both** — so my first hypothesis ("the branch opened the race") was only half right. The **regression is in the *handling***: on `unstable` a lost pin degrades gracefully (the connector drops those keys → treats as a miss/recompute, run continues), whereas on `evolve-dispatcher` a lost pin still gets issued as a load that returns a hard failure, which vLLM asserts on fatally.

So: **yes, it's a regression** — `evolve-dispatcher` converts a previously-survivable eviction race into an engine-killing one. The likely culprit is the load-path / dispatch-map-sharding rework (`perf(dispatcher): batch populate, shard dispatch-map/memory-tier…` / `cut single-key cold load p99`), where a key whose pin was lost is no longer excluded from the subsequent load.

## Now

The `unstable` run is continuing past the race toward a full completion — I'll let it finish to get the clean completed throughput (should land near the prior 5.4 gen/s) and confirm it ends cleanly. ## Turn 80 — Assistant  `2026-09-01 14:58:15 PDT`  _(in:2 out:1,101 cw:6,180 cr:56,831 cost:$0.0567)_

Done. Here's the definitive result.

## Confirmed: it's a regression in `evolve-dispatcher`

The `unstable` confirmation run finished. Both branches were run at the **identical** config — 400 convs, 60 active sessions, async, `/mnt/certus1/synth-1K-M50.json`, 13G memory tier, evict 0.6, on the 4 NUMA-0 SSDs, each with a **freshly-built** `certus-server` for its branch.

| | conv reached | PIN failures | LOAD failures | EngineDead | Result |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **evolve-dispatcher** | crashed @ **121/400** | 12 (one burst) | **12** | **yes** | ✗ `EngineDeadError` — never completes |
| **unstable** | **400/400** | 12 (one burst, @241) | **0** | no | ✓ rc=0, 17418 gens, 3198.8s, **5.4 gen/s** |

### What this tells us

- **The Check→Pin eviction race is pre-existing** — it fires in *both* branches. Under demotion pressure (GPU KV ~98.9%, tier spiked to 99.7%), the background mt-evictor demotes a key in the window between the connector's `Check` (resident) and its `Pin`. `unstable` hit the exact same 12-key burst; the conversation number it lands on is just stochastic.

- **The regression is in the *handling*, not the race.** On `unstable`, a lost pin degrades gracefully: those 12 keys simply don't load (0 LOAD failures, engine survives, run completes). On `evolve-dispatcher`, the same 12 lost pins are no longer excluded from the subsequent load set, so it issues loads that hard-fail → `transfer_result.success=False` → `assert` in vLLM `offloading/worker.py:343` → `EngineDeadError`.

- **Head-to-head throughput:** `unstable` = **5.4 gen/s** completed, which matches the prior head-to-head baseline exactly (validating the harness). `evolve-dispatcher` has **no comparable number** — it crashes before completion, so the earlier apparent cold-path wins in Phase 1 come with the caveat that the branch is not survivable under this async replay workload.

- **Likely culprit:** the load-path rework in `perf(dispatcher): batch populate, shard dispatch-map/memory-tier, tune cold pipeline` (plus the memory-tier/lib.rs +228 and dispatch-map sharding). Sharding plausibly both widened the Check→Pin window and dropped the "exclude lost-pin keys from the load" fallback.

The server itself logged no error in either run — it's purely a client-side check-vs-evict ordering issue, but only `evolve-dispatcher` makes it fatal.

Memory updated (`project_dispatcher_check_pin_eviction_race.md`) from "suspected" to **confirmed**, with the refined mechanism.

The fix would be to restore the graceful-degrade behavior: when a pin is lost, drop those keys from the load set (miss → recompute) instead of issuing a load that returns a hard failure. Want me to locate and draft that fix on `evolve-dispatcher`?

The fix did not resolve the crash. Here's the result.

## `evolve-dispatcher` + your fix: still crashes — same failure mode

Run `evolve-fix-synth1k-async-20260901_151320` (fresh 15:12 binary with `542cd2f9 fix(eviction): shard-targeted eviction for PoolFull retry`), identical config:

| | this run (with fix) | prior evolve crash | unstable baseline |
|---|---|---|---|
| Outcome | ✗ crashed | ✗ crashed | ✓ completed |
| Crashed at | ~36/400 convs (wall 625s) | 121/400 (wall 1185s) | — (400/400) |
| PIN failures | **11** (one burst) | 12 | 12 |
| LOAD failures | **11** | 12 | **0** |
| EngineDead | **yes** (`AssertionError`) | yes | no |
| gen/s | — (no DONE) | — | 5.4 |

The signature is unchanged: a single burst of ~11 lost pins → **every one cascades into a LOAD FAILURE** → `transfer_result.success=False` → `AssertionError` → `EngineDeadError`. Server logged no error and was torn down cleanly.

### Why the fix didn't take

Your commit targets **eviction** (`shard-targeted eviction for PoolFull retry`) — that narrows *which* entries the evictor demotes on a PoolFull retry. But the crash isn't caused by over-eager eviction volume; it's the **Check→Pin ordering race itself** (still fired 11 lost pins) combined with the **fatal handling** of those lost pins. The critical divergence from `unstable` is untouched:

- **unstable:** 12 lost pins → **0 LOAD failures** (the lost-pin keys are dropped from the load set → miss/recompute → survives).
- **evolve-dispatcher (both before and after this fix):** the same lost-pin keys **stay in the load set**, so 11 pins lost → 11 LOADs issued → 11 hard failures → engine dies.

So the fix reduced neither the race window enough to avoid the burst, nor changed the fatal load-path handling. The fix needs to be on the **load path**: when `prepare_load` finds a pin was lost, exclude that key from the load set (as `unstable` does) rather than issuing a load that returns a hard failure.

Want me to diff the load/prepare path between `evolve-dispatcher` and `unstable` (dispatcher + shmq-dispatcher `translate`/`prepare_load`) to pinpoint exactly where evolve stopped dropping lost-pin keys? That's the change that made the race fatal.

## Comparison summary

| Run | Branch / fixes | Outcome | Crash conv | Wall | PIN | LOAD | EngineDead | gen/s |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| baseline | **unstable** | ✓ completed | — (400/400) | 3327s | 12 | **0** | no | **5.4** |
| evolve #1 | evolve-dispatcher (orig) | ✗ crash | 121/400 | 1185s | 12 | 12 | yes | — |
| evolve #2 | + `shard-targeted eviction` | ✗ crash | ~36/400 | 625s | 11 | 11 | yes | — |
| evolve #3 | + `pin-safe eviction`, **`revert sharding`**, `populate store-stream` | ✗ crash | **227/400** | 2078s | 11 | 11 | yes | 5.5 (partial) |

### What this tells us

- **The three fixes helped the *race frequency* but not the *fatal handling*.** The crash moved out from conv 36 → 227, and throughput up to the crash matches `unstable` (5.5 vs 5.4 gen/s). So the sharding revert + pin-safe eviction genuinely reduced how often the Check→Pin window loses a pin — good, but not the actual bug.

- **The defining divergence is untouched.** In every `evolve` variant, a lost pin still maps **1:1 to a LOAD FAILURE** (`PIN=11 → LOAD=11`). On `unstable` the same lost pins produce **`LOAD=0`** — the connector drops those keys from the load set and recomputes. That drop-lost-pin-keys-before-load step is what's missing/broken on `evolve-dispatcher`, and none of the three commits (all server-side: eviction, locks, populate stream) address it.

- **My earlier "sharding is the culprit" call was wrong** — reverting it didn't fix the fatality. The regression lives in the **load path's response to a lost pin**, not the sharding.

The fix needs to be where `prepare_load`/the load set is assembled after pins are taken: any key whose pin came back "gone" must be **excluded from the load** (miss → recompute), never handed to a load that returns `success=False`. Want me to diff that specific path (`shmq-dispatcher` translate/prepare_load and the dispatcher load handler) against `unstable` and pinpoint the exact line where `evolve` stopped dropping lost-pin keys? ## Turn 131 — Assistant  `2026-09-01 17:35:41 PDT`  _(in:2 out:3,221 cw:2,313 cr:130,528 cost:$0.0962)_

Here's where the diff lands. Let me give you the conclusion and the fix I'll make on `evolve-dispatcher-dw`.

## Load-path diff: the regression is isolated

I diffed every load-relevant file against `unstable`. Two things are now certain:

**1. The parts that *could* make a lost pin fatal are byte-identical to unstable:**
- `pin()` — identical
- `check()` — identical
- `batch_lookup()` miss handling — identical (both return `Err(KeyNotFound)` on a miss)
- `memory-tier`, `background.rs` (evictor), `pins.rs`, `dispatch-map` — **no source changes at all** (only docs/specs). Eviction, demotion, and write-through are unchanged, so demoted keys are on SSD and loadable, exactly as on unstable.

**2. The connector cannot help.** `prepare_load` (`manager.py:346`) returns *all* keys — "cannot drop keys from the returned spec (dst block ids are positionally zipped)." So every checked key is loaded regardless of pin result. Survival depends entirely on the server successfully **promoting the evicted key back from SSD** during `lookup`. Unstable does (PIN=12 → LOAD=0). Evolve fails (PIN → LOAD 1:1).

**The only load-path changes are three perf reworks, all in the cold-promotion path:**

| Change | File | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **New single-key inline `promote_and_serve` fast path** | `lib.rs:2254` | Single cold entry now bypasses `ColdReadPool` and promotes inline on the caller thread |
| Cold-read pipeline rewrite (coalesced H2D) + `PIPELINE_RING_SIZE` 8→32 | `pipeline.rs` | Per-object H2D coalescing |
| `QUEUES_PER_DRIVE` 2→1 | `lib.rs:1885/2254` | Halved cold-read queue concurrency |

In async replay, **each vLLM load is a single-key request** — so every concurrent cold load now takes the brand-new inline fast path (#1), which the crash exercises 11-at-once. Unstable routed all cold loads through the serialized `ColdReadPool`. That's the highest-confidence culprit: a survivable cold-miss-then-promote became a hard promotion failure under concurrency/pressure.

## Proposed fix (surgical, testable)

Route single-key cold loads back through the `ColdReadPool` — i.e. remove the inline fast path so evolve's load path matches unstable's promotion behavior, while keeping the other perf wins. If the crash disappears, the fast path is confirmed. Let me make that change and re-run.

This is the smoking gun. The **pooled path has a staging fallback** the inline fast path lacks:

- Lines 2334–2346 + 2411–2413: when the tier is saturated, the pooled path catches `AllocationFailed` and **defers the entry to staging** — "Rather than fail it (which crashes vLLM)... served uncached... so the load still succeeds."
- The single-key inline `promote_and_serve` has **no such fallback**: `evict_and_insert` → `AllocationFailed` → returns `Err` → LOAD FAILURE → crash.

Under 60 concurrent sessions at 99.7% tier occupancy, single-key cold loads hit `AllocationFailed` in bursts. Unstable (pooled path only) stages them; evolve's fast path fails them fatally. That exactly matches the signature. Let me confirm `promote_and_serve`/`batch_device` won't become dead code, then remove the fast path.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Certus: COM-inspired Rust component framework (edition 2021, MSRV 1.75), generative filesystem for inferencing. Dispatcher component provides `IDispatcher` for GPU↔SSD cache with DRAM memory-tier.
   - certus-spdk KV-offload backend = host `certus-server` (SPDK NVMe) + vLLM shmq client container (`localhost/certus-shmq-bench`, podman store `/mnt/certus1/podman/storage`).
   - Tiers: MemoryTier (DRAM, warm) → BlockDevice (SSD, cold). Background mt-evictor demotes DRAM→SSD at eviction threshold 0.6. Cold loads promote SSD→DRAM→GPU.
   - Check→Pin→Lookup protocol: connector Checks a key resident, Pins it (promote=FALSE, eviction-protecting read-ref), then Lookups (H2D load). The race: evictor demotes in Check→Pin window → pin fails.
   - CRITICAL connector constraint: `prepare_load` CANNOT drop keys (dst block ids are positionally zipped) — every checked key is loaded regardless of pin result. Survival depends entirely on the SERVER successfully re-serving (promoting from SSD) the evicted key during lookup.
   - `batch_lookup` classifies entries: warm (MemoryTier hit), cold (BlockDevice/SSD → promote), miss (KeyNotFound), remote. Pooled path (`ColdReadPool`) with per-drive queue threads; had a NEW single-key inline `promote_and_serve` fast path (now removed by me).
   - Staging fallback: pooled path defers `AllocationFailed` (tier saturated) to a staging post-pass, serving uncached — so a cold load never hard-fails under saturation.
   - AlreadyExists concurrent-promotion hazard: two promotes of same key both call mt.insert(); loser gets `MemoryTierError::AlreadyExists` → surfaced as ALLOCATION_FAILED → fails load → crashes vLLM UNLESS a recovery pass re-serves it warm.
   - Replay config: async closed-loop, WORKLOAD_MODE=async, ACTIVE_SESSIONS=60. In async mode each vLLM load is a single-key request.
   - vLLM crash: `assert transfer_result.success` → `AssertionError` → `EngineDeadError` (offloading/worker.py:343, core.py:1332, async_llm.py:704).

**The diagnostic has answered decisively.** The exact fatal error is:

```
kind=IoError err=IoError("remote lookup: key not found")
```

This is the smoking gun. The failure chain is now fully known:

1. Connector Checks key present → Pins it → **PIN FAILURE** (evictor demoted it in the Check→Pin window)
2. Connector loads it anyway (can't drop keys) → server `batch_lookup` classifies it as a **local MISS → `KeyNotFound`**
3. The `KeyNotFound` entry is forwarded to **remote-lookup**, which has nothing → returns "key not found"
4. batch_lookup converts that to **`IoError("remote lookup: key not found")`** → fatal → `assert transfer_result.success` → `EngineDeadError`

The pivotal fact: the demoted key comes back as a **hard `KeyNotFound` miss**, not a promotable SSD (`cold`) entry. On `unstable` the same lost-pin key is served by promoting from SSD (0 LOAD failures); on evolve it's genuinely *gone* locally. Let me capture the crash state, then find why a demoted key becomes a hard miss instead of a cold-SSD hit.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Certus: COM-inspired Rust component framework (edition 2021, MSRV 1.75), generative filesystem for inferencing. Dispatcher component provides `IDispatcher` for GPU↔SSD cache with a DRAM memory-tier.
   - certus-spdk KV-offload backend = host `target/release/certus-server` (SPDK NVMe, 4 drives at PCI 0000:61-64:00.0) + vLLM shmq client container.
   - Tiers: MemoryTier (DRAM, warm) → BlockDevice (SSD, cold). Background mt-evictor demotes DRAM→SSD at eviction threshold 0.6. Cold loads promote SSD→DRAM→GPU.
   - Check→Pin→Lookup protocol: connector Checks a key resident, Pins it (promote=FALSE, eviction-protecting read-ref via `dm.take_read`), then Lookups (H2D load). The race: evictor removes/demotes in the Check→Pin window → pin fails.
   - CRITICAL connector constraint: `prepare_load` CANNOT drop keys (dst block ids positionally zipped) — every Check-hit key is loaded regardless of pin result. Survival depends on the SERVER still successfully serving the key during lookup.
   - `try_evict_to_block` (demote): succeeds ONLY when `Location::MemoryTier { ssd_offset: Some(_) }` (write-through complete) and entry unpinned (read_ref==0, write_ref==0). Sets location to `BlockDevice { offset }` (recoverable). Fails with InvalidState when ssd_offset is None.
   - Write-through: `process_write_job` writes DRAM→SSD, reserves extent, then calls `dm.convert_to_storage(key, offset)` which sets `ssd_offset = Some(offset)` on the MemoryTier entry (line 174-175 of dispatch-map/src/lib.rs).
   - `evict_one_clean` fallback: when try_evict_to_block fails (unpersisted), does `dm.remove(cand)` — a FULL removal → dispatch-map returns NotExist for that key. 2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Certus: COM-inspired Rust component framework (edition 2021, MSRV 1.75). Dispatcher provides `IDispatcher` for GPU↔SSD cache with a DRAM memory-tier.
   - certus-spdk KV-offload backend = host `target/release/certus-server` (SPDK NVMe) + vLLM shmq-connector container (mounted from `/workspace/certus-shmq-connector`; IDENTICAL for both branch runs — only the server binary differs).
   - Tiers: MemoryTier (DRAM) → BlockDevice (SSD). `Location::MemoryTier { pointer, size, ssd_offset }` (ssd_offset=Some means write-through persisted → demotable); `Location::BlockDevice { offset }` (recoverable); NotExist (removed).
   - Content-addressed KV keys: the SAME key is stored/loaded CONCURRENTLY by multiple sessions (same prefix → same KV → same hash key). This enables concurrent lookup/promote/commit on one key.
   - Dispatch-map ref-counting: `read_ref` (pins, allows concurrent readers), `write_ref` (exclusive). `lookup`/`take_read` wait for `write_ref==0`; `take_write` waits for `read_ref==0 && write_ref==0`. `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT = 2000ms`; on timeout → `Err(DispatchMapError::Timeout(key))`.
   - Split-phase store (shmq): Reserve(3)→CopyToStore(4)→CommitStore(5), with AbortStore(6). Opcodes also: CHECK(1), TOUCH(2), PIN(7), UNPIN(9→actually LOOKUP=9), TAKE_EVENTS(10), POPULATE(11), REMOVE(12), CLEAR_MEMORY_TIER(13). `op_check` tri-state MISS(0)/RESIDENT(1)/PENDING(2). Reservation-timeout reaper (`reap_stale_reservations`) reclaims Reserve-without-Commit via `release_memory` + drop pending record.
   - Connector invariants: `_do_store` returns True unconditionally (store failures never crash vLLM). `prepare_load` pins (promote=FALSE via take_read) but CANNOT drop keys from the returned load spec — loads ALL check-hit keys. Only a failed LOAD is fatal (`transfer_result.success=False` → assert → EngineDeadError).
   - Sharding was REVERTED (commit 8f494f8d) — dispatch-map and memory-tier are back to single global-mutex locks, IDENTICAL to unstable.
   - Replay config: async closed-loop, ACTIVE_SESSIONS=60, NUM_CONVS=400, MEM_TIER_SIZE=13G, EVICT_THRESH=0.6, CHANNELS=32, RUNS=1, DATASET_HOST=/mnt/certus1/synth-1K-M50.json, model NousResearch/Meta-Llama-3-8B, TP=1. Crash occurs ~conv 121 (stochastic).

   - `lookup` returns EXACTLY: `Err(Timeout)` (entry exists, write_ref>0 for 2s), `Ok(NotExist)` (entry None), or `Ok(MemoryTier/BlockDevice/MismatchSize)`. Err ⟹ Timeout.

## Turn 372 — Assistant  `2026-09-01 21:15:44 PDT`  _(in:2 out:1,439 cw:644 cr:101,485 cost:$0.0545)_

Everything is landed and validated. Here's the complete picture.

## Regression fixed — `evolve-dispatcher` Check→Pin eviction race

**Committed:** `1d55b9c2` on `evolve-dispatcher-dw` (feature branch, not pushed/merged) — `components/dispatcher/src/lib.rs`, +57/−42 vs `evolve-dispatcher`.

### What actually fixed it (and the correction to the earlier theory)

The prior-session hypothesis was that a lookup **retry** was the fix. **The data disproves that** — 0 `[load-diag]` lines across all 6 runs means no `Timeout`/`NotExist` ever reached classification, so the retry never executed. The real fixes were two evolve-only paths that turned a survivable local miss into a fatal remote-forward:

| # | Change | Fatal path closed | Role |
|---|--------|-------------------|------|
| 1 | `evict_one_clean`: drop `dm.remove` fallback for unpersisted victims | read_ref==0 entry removed → `NotExist` → remote-forward → `EngineDeadError` | real fix |
| 2 | Remove single-key inline cold-load fast path (no staging fallback) | tier-full `AllocationFailed` → fatal LOAD failure | **primary fix** |
| 3 | Bounded retry on lookup `Err` (Timeout ≠ KeyNotFound) | `Err(Timeout)` misread → remote-forward → fatal | defensive only — **never fired** |

### Run-by-run comparison (identical config: 400 convs, 13G mem-tier, evict 0.6, 32 channels, synth-1K-M50, Llama-3-8B, TP=1)

| Binary | Runs clean | gen/s | Crash markers | Diag (retry) fired |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `evolve-dispatcher` (baseline) | **0 / 5** | — (crashed ~conv 121) | `EngineDeadError` ×5 | n/a |
| `evolve-dispatcher-dw` fix (w/ diag) | **5 / 5** | 12.2, 12.2, 12.3, 12.4, 12.2 | 0 | 0 |
| `evolve-dispatcher-dw` cleaned (shipping) | **1 / 1** | 12.5 | 0 | 0 |
| **Fixed total** | **6 / 6** | **~12.2–12.5** | **0** | **0** |

`unstable` was the reference for "correct" behavior: it hits the *same* PIN-failure burst (the race is pre-existing) but degrades gracefully and completes. The fix restores exactly that degrade in `evolve-dispatcher`, while keeping evolve's higher throughput.

### Pre-commit hygiene (all done)
- `[load-diag]` instrumentation removed.
- Unused `read_refs` test helper removed (cleared the `dead_code` warning under `spdk-backend`).
- Release `certus-server` rebuilt — dispatcher + server both recompiled, **zero warnings, zero errors**.
- Only `lib.rs` staged; logs/output dirs left untracked.
- Memory updated to record the fix and the corrected causal story.

The commit sits on the feature branch, unpushed — say the word if you want it pushed or a PR opened against `unstable`.

## Turn 373 — User  `2026-09-02 09:14:49 PDT`

Can you summarize the problem and fix

---

## Turn 373 — Assistant  `2026-09-02 09:15:08 PDT`  _(in:43 out:1,026 cw:103,582 cr:0 cost:$0.4040)_

## The problem

The certus-spdk KV-offload replay crashed vLLM on branch `evolve-dispatcher`, but ran clean on `unstable`.

**The race (pre-existing in both branches):** The vLLM connector *Checks* that a KV key is resident, decides to load it, then *Pins* and loads it. Under memory-tier pressure, the background evictor can act on that same content-addressed key in the Check→Pin window. So a key that was "present" at Check time is gone by load time.

**The regression (evolve-only):** `unstable` survives this — a lost pin degrades to a cache miss and the run completes. `evolve-dispatcher` turned the *same* lost-pin condition **fatal**: the load hard-failed → `transfer_result.success=False` → assertion → `EngineDeadError`. Result: 0/5 replay runs completed.

## The cause

Two evolve-only code paths converted a survivable local miss into a **fatal remote-forward** (a local miss gets forwarded to remote-lookup, whose miss the connector treats as fatal):

1. **`evict_one_clean`** — a fallback did a full `dm.remove` on an unpersisted victim. A just-Checked-but-not-yet-Pinned entry has `read_ref==0`, so the remove succeeded and dropped it entirely → `NotExist` → fatal.
2. **Cold-load fast path** — a single-key inline `promote_and_serve` had *no staging fallback*, so when the tier was full, `AllocationFailed` became a fatal load failure. **This was the primary cause.**

## The fix

`components/dispatcher/src/lib.rs` (commit `1d55b9c2` on `evolve-dispatcher-dw`, +57/−42):

1. **`evict_one_clean`**: never full-remove — demotion keeps the key resolvable (as BlockDevice); skip the victim, else return `AllocationFailed` and serve uncached.
2. **Cold load**: route all cold entries through the pooled path, which defers tier saturation to a staging post-pass (uncached serve instead of fatal).
3. **Classification (defensive)**: bounded retry so a lookup `Err` (a transient write_ref *timeout*) isn't misread as `KeyNotFound`.

**Notable correction:** the earlier theory was that the retry (#3) was the fix. Validation disproved it — **0 diagnostic lines across 6 runs** show the retry never fired. The real fixes are **#1 and #2**; #3 is kept only as correctness defense for a genuine-but-unobserved path.

## Validation

Identical replay (400 convs, 13G tier, evict 0.6, 32 channels, synth-1K-M50, Llama-3-8B, TP=1):

- **Fixed code: 6/6 clean** (5-run confirm + 1 shipping-binary confirm) at ~12.2–12.5 gen/s
- **Prior `evolve-dispatcher`: 0/5 clean**

Committed on the feature branch only — unpushed, no PR yet.

## Turn 374 — Assistant  `2026-09-02 09:19:26 PDT`  _(in:2 out:4,562 cw:1,076 cr:103,582 cost:$0.1035)_

To explain the fixes, you first need the piece of machinery both of them hinge on: **how a local cache miss becomes a fatal crash.** Once that's clear, each fix is just "stop manufacturing that specific miss."

## The shared mechanism: the "fatal funnel"

The dispatch-map is a single global `Mutex<HashMap<CacheKey, DispatchEntry>>` + `Condvar`. Each entry carries two reference counts:
- `read_ref` — concurrent readers/pins (an in-flight load holds one)
- `write_ref` — exclusive writer (a store-commit or promote holds one)

`dm.lookup(key)` has exactly three outcomes:
- `Ok(MemoryTier{…} / BlockDevice{…})` — a hit; the key is resolvable
- `Ok(NotExist)` — the entry is **gone** (the HashMap slot is `None`)
- `Err(Timeout)` — the entry *exists* but a writer held `write_ref` for the full 2000 ms wait

In the load classification loop, both `Ok(NotExist)` and (in the old code) `Err(_)` were turned into `DispatcherError::KeyNotFound`. Those KeyNotFound keys are then **collected and forwarded to remote-lookup** (`batch_lookup`, ~line 2575). On this single-node setup there is no remote peer, so the forward fails:

```
KeyNotFound → remote batch_lookup → DispatcherError::IoError("remote lookup: key not found")
           → connector sees transfer_result.success = False
           → assert in vllm offloading/worker.py → EngineDeadError  (whole engine dies)
```

That's the funnel. **Anything that makes a live key look like `NotExist` (or a non-hit) at classification time is fatal**, because the remote-forward path has no graceful degrade — it cannot "just recompute." `unstable` never crashes because it never feeds a live key into this funnel. `evolve-dispatcher` had two paths that did. That's the whole regression.

Critically: eviction *by itself* is safe. The memory-tier evictor (`mt-evictor`) is **demote-only** — `try_evict_to_block` flips the entry in place from `MemoryTier` to `BlockDevice`, keeping the entry present and resolvable. A demoted key still returns `Ok(BlockDevice)`, still resolves via a cold read. The danger is only in code that turns an entry into `None` or into an unrecoverable failure.

---

## Fix #1 — `evict_one_clean`: stop full-removing unpersisted victims

**The code.** When the memory tier needs to free a slot, `evict_one_clean` picks a victim. If the victim's write-through to SSD is complete (it has an `ssd_offset`), it demotes in place — safe, entry survives as `BlockDevice`. But the old code had a **fallback for victims whose write-through hadn't landed yet** (no `ssd_offset`, so nothing to demote *to*):

```rust
// OLD — the fatal fallback
if dm.remove(cand).is_ok() {          // <- turns the entry into None
    let _ = mt.remove(cand);
    self.emit_eviction(cand, EvictionReason::Removed);
    return true;
}
```

**Why it was fatal.** `dm.remove` deliberately *refuses* to remove a pinned entry (`read_ref > 0`) — the author's intent was "a success means no in-flight load points at it, so it's safe to free." But that reasoning has a hole under the Check→Pin race: a connector that has just *Checked* a key resident but hasn't *Pinned* it yet has `read_ref == 0`. So `dm.remove` sees an unpinned entry, succeeds, and silently drops it. The connector then Pins and loads → `lookup` returns `Ok(NotExist)` → straight into the fatal funnel.

The `read_ref == 0` guard is a proxy for "nobody cares about this key," but there's a window where somebody *does* care and just hasn't taken their pin yet. Removal is irreversible; the connector has no way to recover a key that vanished.

**The fix.** Delete the fallback entirely — never full-remove here:

```rust
// NEW — skip instead of remove
// Write-through incomplete so it can't be demoted. Do NOT full-remove:
// a just-Checked-but-not-yet-Pinned entry has read_ref==0, so dm.remove
// would succeed and silently drop it → fatal miss. Skip this candidate;
// the widening scan will reach a persisted (demotable) entry.
```

The reasoning behind why this is *safe to skip*:
- Demotion leaves the key resolvable; a full remove does not. So we simply never do the unrecoverable thing.
- `evict_for_space` widens its scan across candidates, so it reaches a **persisted** (therefore demotable) entry and frees space that way.
- If the *entire* tier is unpersisted (nothing demotable), `evict_one_clean` returns `false` → the caller gets `AllocationFailed` → it serves **uncached** rather than crashing. A slow serve, not a dead engine.
- An unpersisted entry becomes demotable the instant its background write-through completes, so this is a transient, self-clearing condition.

Net: we trade a rare "couldn't free a slot this instant" (survivable) for eliminating an unrecoverable data loss (fatal).

---

## Fix #2 — cold load: remove the no-staging fast path (the primary fix)

**The code.** When keys miss the memory tier and must be read from SSD ("cold load"), the normal path is a pooled worker that promotes-and-serves with an important safety net: if the memory tier is full, promotion returns `Err(AllocationFailed)`, and the pooled path **catches it and stages** instead:

```rust
Err(AllocationFailed) if staging_available => staged.push(ci),  // degrade: serve uncached
```

But evolve had added a **latency optimization**: for the common single-key, single-region case (`bs=1`), skip the pool thread hop and call `promote_and_serve` **inline**:

```rust
// OLD — inline fast path, NO staging fallback
if cold_entries.len() == 1 && cold_entries[0].regions.len() == 1 {
    let res = self.promote_and_serve(entry.key, …);  // Err(AllocationFailed) here is FATAL
    results[entry.idx] = Some(res);
}
```

**Why it was fatal.** The inline path shaved two context switches — a genuine latency win, the reason it was added ([[project_dispatcher]] cold-load work, commit c410ac44 cut single-key p99 by 57%). But it had no staging fallback. Under memory-tier pressure — exactly the replay's steady state, with the tier at ~99% and the evictor demoting on a ~200 ms loop — a single-key cold load hits `promote_and_serve` → tier is full → `Err(AllocationFailed)` → that error is returned directly as the load result → fatal.

So the fast path took the *most common* load shape (one key at a time) and, under pressure, converted "tier is momentarily full" (survivable everywhere else) into a hard failure. This is why it was the dominant crash source — it fired on ordinary single-key loads, not just on the rarer eviction-race timing.

**The fix.** Route *all* cold entries — including the lone single-key case — through the pooled path:

```rust
// NEW — everything goes through the pooled path (with staging fallback)
// The single-key inline fast path is intentionally NOT used: it has no
// staging fallback, so under memory-tier pressure it turned a survivable
// cold miss into a fatal LOAD FAILURE. The scheduling win is not worth
// reintroducing that failure mode.
if !cold_entries.is_empty() { … pooled path … }
```

We give back the ~57% single-key latency optimization. That's a deliberate correctness-over-speed trade: the pooled path's `AllocationFailed → stage → serve uncached` is the *only* thing standing between "tier full" and "engine dead." A faster load that occasionally kills vLLM is not faster in any meaningful sense.

---

## Fix #3 — classification retry: defensive, and provably not the fix

**The code.** In the load classification loop, the old code collapsed *any* `dm.lookup` `Err` into `KeyNotFound` — feeding `Err(Timeout)` into the fatal funnel. But `Err(Timeout)` is semantically **not** a missing key: it means "the entry exists, but a writer held `write_ref` past the 2 s wait." The key is alive; the writer will release. The fix retries the local lookup a few times so the transient `write_ref` clears before giving up:

```rust
const LOOKUP_RETRIES: u32 = 5;
let mut lk = dm.lookup(key);
let mut retries = 0u32;
while matches!(lk, Err(_)) && retries < LOOKUP_RETRIES {
    retries += 1;
    lk = dm.lookup(key);
}
```

**Why it's defensive-only.** This is a genuine latent bug — `Timeout ≠ KeyNotFound`, and misclassifying it *would* be fatal — but the validation data shows **it never fired**: 0 `[load-diag]` lines across all 6 runs, meaning no `Timeout` (and no `NotExist`) ever reached classification. That makes mechanistic sense: with the integrity-check CRC gated off, a writer's `write_ref` window is sub-millisecond (`create_memory_tier_entry` → `downgrade_reference`), so a 2 s wait effectively never expires. The write_ref-held-for-2s scenario requires pathological lock contention that this workload doesn't produce.

I kept it anyway because it's a *correct* fix for a *real* misclassification, and it guards the one remaining way a live key could reach the fatal funnel if contention ever did widen that window. But I was careful in the commit and the comment to say plainly: **fixes #1 and #2 are what the 6/6 clean runs demonstrate; #3 is insurance, not the cure.** The earlier session had it backwards — it thought the retry was the fix — and the diagnostic instrumentation (now removed) is precisely what proved otherwise.

---

## The common thread

All three are the same principle: **a cache is allowed to miss, but a KV load must never turn a live key into a hard failure.** `unstable` honored that implicitly; evolve's three optimizations (aggressive eviction reclaim, inline cold-load, terse error mapping) each broke it in a different spot. The fixes restore the invariant — degrade to demote, to staging, or to a retry — while keeping evolve's throughput (~12.2–12.5 gen/s, well above unstable's graceful-degrade baseline).

## Steps

1. Find the most recent session JSONL. Try exact project key first, then fall back to the most recently modified JSONL across all projects (which is almost certainly this session):

```bash
PROJECT_KEY=$(pwd | sed 's|/|-|g')
JSONL=$(ls -t ~/.claude/projects/${PROJECT_KEY}/*.jsonl 2>/dev/null | head -1)
if [[ -z "$JSONL" ]]; then
    JSONL=$(find ~/.claude/projects/ -maxdepth 2 -name '*.jsonl' -printf '%T@ %p\n' 2>/dev/null | sort -rn | head -1 | cut -d' ' -f2-)
fi
if [[ -z "$JSONL" ]]; then
    echo "No session JSONL found" >&2
    exit 1
fi
echo "JSONL: $JSONL"
```

2. Determine output path — use the argument if provided, otherwise save to current directory. If the argument is a directory, create the transcript file inside it:

```bash
SESSION_ID=$(basename "$JSONL" .jsonl)
DATE=$(date +%Y-%m-%d)
DEFAULT_NAME="transcript_${SESSION_ID}_${DATE}.md"
if [[ -n "/home/dwaddington/certus/components/dispatcher/info" ]]; then
    if [[ -d "/home/dwaddington/certus/components/dispatcher/info" ]]; then
        OUT="${ARGUMENTS%/}/${DEFAULT_NAME}"
    else
        OUT="/home/dwaddington/certus/components/dispatcher/info"
    fi
else
    OUT="$(pwd)/${DEFAULT_NAME}"
fi
```

3. Find and run the save script relative to the repo root:

```bash
SAVE_SCRIPT="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel 2>/dev/null)/.claude/skills/save/save.sh"
if [[ ! -f "$SAVE_SCRIPT" ]]; then
    echo "save.sh not found at: $SAVE_SCRIPT" >&2
    exit 1
fi
bash "$SAVE_SCRIPT" "$JSONL" "$OUT"
```

4. Report the output path and total estimated cost.
